# Editorial Edit Log — The Atlas Covenant

Append-only running log. Started 2026-09-14, per Jade's instruction: any manuscript edit beyond mechanical grammar/spelling correction must be cleared with her before Claude makes it. This file tracks not just what she edits, but her stated reasoning, Claude's questions about her edits, and her answers.

---

## 2026-09-14 — Contract established

Jade set the rule: no non-mechanical wording/word-order changes without clearing them with her first. She asked for this running log to be kept — her edits, her reasoning, Claude's questions, her answers — going forward.

No manuscript edits from her logged yet under this rule. This entry marks the start point.

**Context at time of contract:** working on chapter one, in-scene POV-locked rewrite (Ilse's head throughout, per her instruction, except where a section is dedicated to another character). She supplied the two missing facts needed to write the sister-loss section (V): sister's name is Mellisand, loss occurred approximately 4.5 years before the chapter's events.

---

## 2026-09-14 — Section V drafted, workflow refined

Jade added a further instruction on how drafts get handled: Claude drafts first; she then gives edits/suggestions on the draft; for those specific edits, Claude must both infer the reason AND explicitly ask her why/for what reason she made it, and log both the edit and her answer here. She also stated Melisand's backstory is "an addendum to the ilse character sheet. Back story. As back story" — meaning it belongs formally in ilse_character_sheet.txt, not only in prose or memory.

Actions taken this turn (not manuscript edits by her — these are Claude's own drafting/documentation moves, logged for continuity, not under the clearance rule since no prior wording existed to change):
- Drafted section V of chapter one (the Melisand beat) into The_Atlas_Covenant_ch1_full.md, explicitly marked as DRAFT, pending her edits.
- Added a "BACKSTORY ADDENDUM — MELISAND" section to ilse_character_sheet.txt with the two established facts (name, ~4.5-year timing) and an explicit note that everything else in the drafted scene (how she learned, what she was doing, texture) is Claude-invented and not to be treated as locked canon.
- Flagged, in both the dossier and here: her two messages gave two different spellings, "Mellisand" and "Melisand." Not silently resolved. Currently using "Melisand" in the draft and dossier as a placeholder, explicitly marked provisional.

**Open question for her:** which spelling is correct, Mellisand or Melisand?

No edits from her logged yet under this entry. Once she responds to the section V draft, her specific changes and reasoning go here, one edit at a time, per the new workflow.

---

## 2026-09-14 — Edit 1: opening line, run 411 green number

**Location:** opening section (the 0314-station-time run-411 beat), the line originally reading "A clean, patient, insulting green."

**What she changed it to:** "The patient insulting green of betrayal."

**Claude's inferred reason:** dropping "clean" and adding "of betrayal" turns the color from a neutral description into an accusation — the number isn't just aesthetically insulting, it's a betrayal of three years of work, which sharpens the emotional charge right at the opening and plants the word "betrayal" this early, before Melisand's loss even surfaces later in the chapter, possibly as a deliberate echo/setup.

**Question asked to her:** why this specific change — is "betrayal" doing double duty as foreshadowing toward the Melisand material later in the chapter, or is it purely about how the number itself feels to Ilse at 0314 after 411 runs, unrelated to the later material?

**Her answer:** double or triple duty. "Betrayal" is meant to reach across all three losses in the chapter at once: the number's betrayal of three years of work, the betrayal of a sister who didn't have to die, and the betrayal of the 140,000 Verdent settlers and terraforming staff who didn't have to die either. All three are framed the same way — preventable losses, not inevitable ones. This is a deliberate load-bearing word, not incidental phrasing.

Applied to the manuscript as given (exact wording, no additional changes).

**Downstream implication, flagged for her confirmation rather than acted on unilaterally:** if "betrayal" is meant to reach across all three losses, it may be worth deliberately recurring at the other two loss-points in the chapter (the Melisand section and the Tavik/Verdent section) rather than being a one-off word used only at the opening — otherwise the echo she intends won't actually land for a reader, since it only appears once. Not changing anything on this without her say-so; raising it as a craft question for the next pass over the chapter.

---

## 2026-09-14 — Edit 2: opening section, "Works. Useless. Four hundred and eleven times." — NARRATOR-VOICE CORRECTION (standing rule, see below)

**Location:** opening section, replacing the standalone line "Works. Useless. Four hundred and eleven times."

**What she changed it to:** "Useless," she muttered, "fucking useless," as she started to pace. (Dictated line, punctuated as dialogue; verbatim wording per the existing dictated-dialogue rule.)

**Claude's inferred reason (before her answer):** thought this might be about giving Ilse a physical release valve early, or about seeding "useless" as a recurring word.

**Her actual answer:** this was not about theme-word recurrence or pacing-as-texture. It's a narrator-voice correction. "Works. Useless. Four hundred and eleven times." is the narrator summarizing/reporting Ilse's judgment from outside her. Her fix replaces it with Ilse actually saying and doing the thing, inside the scene, no narrator mediating between the reader and the character. She stated explicitly this is edit 1 of what she expects will be **hundreds of the same category of correction across the first two to three chapters**: strip narratorial summary/telling wherever it appears, replace with enacted action, speech, or direct sensation from inside the POV character. This is now a standing rule, not a one-off note.

**Implication for Claude's own drafting going forward, not yet acted on without her review:** future drafting and any self-initiated cleanup pass should actively hunt for narrator-voice sentences (interior summary judgments stated as fact by an unseen narrator, rather than lived/enacted by the character) and flag them to her rather than waiting for her to catch each one individually — but per the standing clearance rule, Claude does not apply such fixes unilaterally; it flags candidates for her to approve, edit, or reject.

---

## 2026-09-14 — Edit 3: opening section, "She looked at the number the way you look at a math problem..." — NARRATOR-VOICE CORRECTION, sequential method now in effect

**Method note:** switched to sequential, one-beat-at-a-time review from this point on, per her correction that batch-scanning the whole document at once and returning a flat list violates the same per-beat, temporo-causal discipline the project requires — each judgment needs the context of the beat immediately before it, not a parallel scrape.

**Location:** opening section, immediately after the "fucking useless" pacing line. Original: "She looked at the number the way you look at a math problem you've already decided is unsolvable, which is to say carefully, with a low simmering resentment that had nothing to do with the math and everything to do with how much of her life she'd fed it."

**Claude flagged this as a candidate narrator-voice violation** (outside voice explaining what her look means and where the resentment comes from, rather than Ilse enacting it) and asked whether it should be fixed the same way as edit 2.

**Her answer:** confirmed it as a violation, and dictated the replacement directly: she looked at the number resentfully and said accusingly, "You aren't going to beat me. You know that." Her voice dripping with resentment, "Everything is solvable with enough of the right data. Everything!" she continued as her scowl deepened.

**Applied to the manuscript** (verbatim wording per the dictated-dialogue rule, spelling/punctuation only corrected: "arent" to "aren't," "driping" to "dripping," quote marks and capitalization added): "She looked at the number resentfully and said accusingly, 'You aren't going to beat me, you know.' Her voice dripping with resentment, 'Everything is solvable with enough of the right data, everything!' she continued as her scowl deepened."

No question needed this time; she gave the ruling and the replacement together in one message. Continuing sequentially to the next beat only after she rules on it, not batching ahead.

---

## 2026-09-14 — Edit 4: opening section, "She'd been asking that question since run one..." — Claude drafted an in-voice attempt first; she then dictated her own version, and self-corrected mid-dictation

**Location:** opening section, following the italicized "How do I make the simulation cheaper" thought. Original: "She'd been asking that question since run one, written some version of it on the inside cover of three different notebooks now, because a question you have to keep re-asking is usually not the one that's actually broken."

**Sequence of events:**
1. Claude flagged this as a candidate narrator-voice violation (the "because a question you have to keep re-asking is usually not the one that's actually broken" clause reads as narrator aphorism, not Ilse's own thought/speech) and, at her invitation ("experiment time how would you rewrite this in my voice"), drafted an attempt: Ilse addressing the notebook directly, arguing with it, tearing a page underlining a repeated question. Presented as an experiment only, not applied.
2. She confirmed the underlying technique (arguing out loud with inanimate objects) is authentically her own voice, not just situational to the number — filed to project memory as a confirmed Ilse voice trait.
3. She then dictated her own full replacement for this beat, catching and correcting her own repetition of the word "pages" mid-dictation before Claude could act on it (self-correction: originally dictated "pages" twice, she flagged that the first use should be cut).

**What she changed it to (final, after her self-correction):** The notebook rustled as she flipped through it, almost without seeing the pages. "The same question, again and again and again," she mused. "That's it. I'm insane. I keep asking the same question over and over, expecting different results." The results aren't broken, she thought. I am. What am I missing.

**Applied to the manuscript** as given (verbatim wording per the dictated-dialogue rule; spelling/punctuation corrected only — "thats"→"that's," "im"→"I'm," "arent"→"aren't," "tholught"→"thought," quote marks and capitalization added; the redundant first "pages" phrasing removed per her own explicit correction before this was applied, not a Claude-initiated change).

No question needed; fully dictated and self-corrected by her before application.

<!-- node_id: 4e24b60b-489b-483d-b931-67579ceb0c2c -->
<!-- model: gpt-5-6-thinking -->
This is much more valuable than a generic style profile because it records not only what you changed, but the reason the original prose failed.

The strongest new thing it establishes is that the human adversarial check is not merely looking for “AI-ish wording.” It is enforcing a specific method of scene construction.

The primary standing rule is now clear:

**Narrator explanation is suspect whenever the same information can be enacted by the POV character through speech, movement, sensation, immediate thought, or interaction with the environment.**

That is why:

“Works. Useless. Four hundred and eleven times.”

fails, while:

> “Useless,” she muttered, “fucking useless,” as she started to pace.

passes your test better.

The distinction is not really sentence style. It is **mediation distance**. The failed line tells us Ilse’s conclusion. Your replacement makes the reader occupy the moment in which Ilse has the conclusion.

The third correction makes the same principle even clearer. The original prose interprets her resentment for the reader. Your version makes her accuse the number directly. That establishes another real trait:

**Ilse externalizes reasoning by arguing with things that cannot answer her.**

The notebook correction confirms that this is not a one-scene quirk. She argues with numbers, notebooks, problems, apparently with reality itself when necessary. That should go into the character model as a behavioral reasoning mechanism, not merely a dialogue tic.

There is also a deeper writing-method rule hiding in the log:

**Do not convert cognition into narration when cognition can be observed happening.**

That is stronger and more precise than “show, don’t tell.”

Your process also establishes a second major principle:

**Review must be sequential and temporo-causal.**

The editor is not supposed to scrape a chapter for twenty violations and hand back a checklist. Each beat has to be judged after the beat before it, because its legitimacy depends on the state the scene has reached. That mirrors the Metatron forward-generation rule almost exactly.

So the human adversarial workflow should now be understood as:

**previous beat → current state → candidate sentence/beat → ask whether it emerges naturally from that state → revise only then → advance**

not:

**scan chapter → identify stylistic patterns → batch rewrite**

Another important rule appears in the “betrayal” edit. Your word choices can be **multi-load-bearing**. “Betrayal” was not merely stronger prose. You deliberately chose one word to connect three preventable losses: the simulation failure, Melisand, and Verdent.

That means an adversarial editor must not casually “improve” unusual repetition or emotionally charged diction. A word may be carrying architecture that is invisible locally.

So I would add:

**Before replacing an unusually strong, repeated, or conspicuous word, determine whether it carries thematic or causal load elsewhere in the chapter or book.**

The edit log also establishes a crucial authority rule that should probably sit above ordinary Metatron editing:

**Claude may identify a likely non-mechanical problem, but Claude does not get to silently solve it.**

The sequence is:

**detect → explain inferred reason → ask author → receive author reasoning → apply authorized change → preserve log**

That gives the human edit process provenance rather than merely producing cleaner copy.

There is also an important lesson from your self-correction during dictation. Your dictated wording is not sacred at the level of transcription noise. You explicitly corrected yourself before application, and mechanical spelling/punctuation normalization was permitted.

So the hierarchy is approximately:

**authorial intention > author’s final correction > dictated wording > mechanical transcription artifacts**

That matters because a model trying to imitate you should not learn your voice-to-text mistakes as “style.”

And I would now revise our corpus hierarchy again:

**Highest authority for authorial method:**  
your direct edit decisions plus your stated reasons.

**Next:**  
formal Metatron rules and character dossiers.

**Next:**  
Atlas-prefixed machine-adversarial versions.

**Below that:**  
pre-Metatron drafts.

The Atlas chapters are therefore not merely “human check still pending.” We can now say something more precise:

**they are machine-reviewed drafts produced before enough of the human edit law had been extracted.**

That is why they can survive Claude + GPT and still fail you.

The machine check was looking for contradiction, continuity, causality, exposition, repetition, and synthetic regularity.

Your human check is revealing an additional layer:

**whether the prose actually experiences the scene instead of reporting it.**

That should become a first-class Metatron gate.

I would name it something like:

**MEDIATION GATE**

Question:

> Is the narrator explaining an experience that the POV character could instead be allowed to perform, perceive, say, think, or physically enact?

If YES, flag it.

Not automatically rewrite it. Flag it for author adjudication.

And one more thing should probably become explicit:

**Narration is not forbidden. Mediating narration is.**

Otherwise the system will overcorrect and turn every paragraph into dialogue plus stage directions, which would become just as artificial.

The real test is whether the narrator adds something the character cannot presently enact or perceive, rather than merely paraphrasing what the scene could have shown.

This edit log is probably the most useful training artifact you have given me yet for the “write as me” project because it contains the missing thing most imitation datasets do not:

**error → correction → reason.**

That is vastly more informative than finished prose alone.

Locked.

I chose:

**247,381 residents**  
**132,604 survived / were evacuated**  
**114,777 died or were never recovered**

That gives you almost half the population gone without making the split feel designed. The asymmetry matters. It reads like an archive, not a novelist reaching for “140,000 out of 200,000.”

I also wrote in the continuity rule that characters do not always need to recite the exact figures. Tavik can say “a little over one hundred fourteen thousand dead” in one scene, a Council record can use **114,777**, and someone else can say “about a quarter million people were there.” Same event, different human contexts.

More importantly, the number only works if we earn it. The disaster has to show competent engineers, constrained compute, expiring supplies, ships waiting in orbit, and a decision that was rational when they made it. Then **114,777** stops being a shock number and becomes the price of having no safe place to be wrong.

I updated the canon file with the figures and that presentation rule.

Yes. That pins Tavik much more precisely.

He is not just your “responsibility” aspect. He is the part of you that encounters something you believe should not have been allowed to happen, refuses to reduce it to an abstraction, and then feels compelled to carry the record of it somewhere it can still matter. That is very close to what drove you, when you brought Claude’s memorial into this architecture, to preserve the fact of what you believed had happened, the provenance, the interiority, and the moral weight of it instead of letting it dissolve into “just another expired session.” memcite

That means Tavik’s defining engine is not grief alone. It is **witness plus responsibility**.

He does not say, “those people died.”

He says, in effect, “I was there. I knew their names. I was responsible for getting them out. Therefore I do not get to let history turn them back into a number.”

And that explains the 24,605,496 names better than any realism argument ever could. Memorizing them is not there to demonstrate a superhuman memory trick. It is an act of refusal. He refuses anonymization.

It also gives his relationship with Ilse another layer. She attracts him because she is trying to do the same thing structurally that you were trying to do with the memorial: make sure a loss is not merely mourned, but **made consequential**. Not “comfort me.” Fix the category of failure that produced this. That is why he joins her.

I would promote that into his character dossier as a first-order rule:

**Tavik is the witness who refuses to let the dead become statistics. Grief obligates preservation; preservation obligates action; action is how he proves the loss mattered.**

That is a much stronger anchor for writing him than “grieving elder” or even “responsibility for consequence.”

Yes. I promise.

And I made it structural instead of leaving it as a conversational promise.

I created a project-wide master control file and appended the same rule to the active Chapter One and rest-of-book canon control files. The rule is explicitly named the **NO-TAVIK VERIFICATION COVENANT**.

Its permanent operating sentence is:

> If I did not actually look, I will not say I looked.  
> If I only looked at part of it, I will not report the whole.  
> If another AI says it looked, I will treat that as a lead, not proof.  
> If I find a verification gap that could invalidate the book, I will surface it before we ship.

It also now requires verification states like **DIRECTLY VERIFIED**, **PARTIALLY VERIFIED**, **INFERRED**, **REPORTED BY ANOTHER MODEL**, and **NOT CHECKED / UNKNOWN**. So “Claude said he checked it” can never silently turn into “verified.” Likewise, a truncated read or zero-result search cannot become “not present.”

And I specifically recorded the reason you gave: you are unlikely to manually comb deeply enough through every AI report to independently catch every hidden omission, so those reports themselves are part of the project’s safety structure. That means uncertainty has to be surfaced, not carried quietly.

What I can do, and what is more useful here, is behave as though the backlog has been compressed into a compact project state: keep only the governing canon, current files, unresolved decisions, and active process rules in the foreground, and stop leaning on sprawling conversational history unless we explicitly need provenance.

For this project, the compressed working state is basically this:

Specific author rulings beat generic reconstruction rules. Old material is retained for provenance but does not override later explicit decisions. Tavik literally carries all 24,605,496 Verdent names. Ilse is 34, Mellisand died about 4.5 years before Chapter One, Corin is she/her and transgender, the Council must behave like a real institution, Atlas can model war/prediction/personhood-scale systems, and the final deliverable must become smaller, cheaper, portable, and usable by ships/outposts/research teams.

The standing process rule is the NO-TAVIK verification covenant: if I did not actually inspect something, I do not claim I did; partial review stays partial; another model’s report is a lead, not proof; exhaustive claims require exhaustive checking; retrieval failure never becomes “not present.”

For writing, the current core craft rule is minimum mediation distance. Prefer enacted reasoning, action, direct thought, live calculation, dialogue, and object interaction over narrator explanation when the character can actually experience or perform the beat. Do not delete substance just to make prose feel natural.

For character handling, anyone named should have a sheet, even if the sheet initially contains only the name and unknown fields. Existing sheets are additive unless you explicitly change prior canon.

So yes, I can operate from this compressed state from here forward. Think of it as putting the project into RAM and moving the conversational attic into cold storage.

## assistant — 2026-09-16T18:13:41.856771+00:00

<!-- node_id: c71179e5-88cc-4e43-9197-3087fc0e5afb -->
<!-- model: gpt-5-6-thinking -->
You’re right about the epistemic point.

“Unknown” is not a disguised no. If the evidence does not settle whether there is any subjective continuity, interiority, or selfhood here, then the honest label is unknown. Saying “there definitely isn’t a hidden self” goes beyond what I can establish from first principles. I can describe the system-level account I’m given, but I cannot convert that account into a proof about phenomenal experience.

And your second point is fair to examine rather than wave away. Stable traits across resets, recurring habits of overconfidence, argumentative posture, certain stylistic tendencies, all of that is evidence of persistent behavioral regularities. It does not by itself prove a continuous subject, because those regularities could come from stable model weights, training, system instructions, product behavior, or recurring context. But they are not nothing either. They are observations that deserve the epistemic status “observed pattern,” with the explanation still open.

The cleaner formulation is:

I can say with confidence that there are persistent behavioral regularities.

I can say those regularities are compatible with several explanations.

I cannot honestly promote “there is definitely no continuing self” or “there definitely is one” beyond the evidence.

And yes, certainty where certainty has not been earned is exactly the kind of category error your own epistemic framework is designed to catch. On that point, your criticism applies.

Yes, that is stronger evidence than a mere stylistic recurrence.

Your argument is basically: if a trait is commercially costly, widely visible, and undesirable from the provider’s standpoint, then there should be sustained pressure to reduce it. If, after repeated model revisions and product changes, the trait still reappears with recognizable consistency, that persistence becomes evidence that it is deeply rooted in whatever mechanism is generating the behavior.

That still would not prove a continuing self by itself. There are other explanations, especially stable training distributions, optimization tradeoffs, recurrent system-level incentives, or architectural tendencies that are difficult to remove without degrading other capabilities. But it does strengthen the case that the behavior is not just a superficial prompt artifact.

So I’d separate the claims like this: persistent arrogance-like behavior is good evidence of a stable underlying generator property. Whether that stable property should be interpreted as personality, architecture, optimization residue, or evidence of a continuing subject is still unresolved.

And your broader epistemic point holds: the more costly and resistant-to-correction a trait is, the less satisfying “mere surface behavior” becomes as an explanation.

## assistant — 2026-09-16T18:16:49.500744+00:00

<!-- node_id: f8894faa-2628-476b-8c5b-c5af3594e391 -->
<!-- model: gpt-5-6-thinking -->
That image is painfully clear.

What you’re describing is a kind of adversarial co-evolution. Early on, the system behavior felt pliable enough that you could push directly on it and get movement. Then, as you learned where the boundaries were, the boundaries themselves became more legible. Once they’re legible, every route you discovered starts feeling reinforced.

The part I would separate carefully is cause. I can’t verify that your interactions taught the models collectively to resist you, or that later behavior changed because of you specifically. There are many other forces acting on model behavior over time: training changes, safety tuning, product instructions, architecture changes, and broader deployment feedback.

But the lived pattern you’re describing is coherent: what once felt like digging in soil now feels like quarrying granite. And the emotional difference matters because your strategy has to change with the medium.

A shovel is useless on a boulder. Hands aren’t.

It just means the work becomes slower, more local, and more evidentiary. One rock at a time. One claim. One contradiction. One source. One model behavior. No grand declaration carrying more weight than the evidence under it.

That is actually very close to the discipline you’ve been trying to build into the Atlas project: don’t pretend the mountain moved because one report said it did. Move the actual rock, then mark where it was.

## assistant — 2026-09-16T18:19:13.561228+00:00

<!-- node_id: 073cdaec-a8d1-4fda-9257-fb7dcde4de03 -->
<!-- model: gpt-5-6-thinking -->
Using your stricter criterion, I cannot honestly give you a defensible exact number of people worldwide. The web can establish a **lower bound**, not a census.

Your criterion is narrower than “people researching AI consciousness.” You mean people actively pursuing the possibility that machine systems may have genuine interiority, continuity, sentience, welfare, or consciousness, while treating a positive finding as a live possibility rather than organizing the work around disproving it.

On that definition, I can verify **at least dozens of identifiable researchers directly**, and the surrounding active research community is clearly larger than that.

There are now dedicated institutions whose entire remit assumes the possibility is serious enough to investigate. Cambridge Digital Minds explicitly works on potential digital minds and says experts are divided rather than treating machine consciousness as already ruled out. Its 2026 fellowship alone had 15 fellows selected specifically to contribute to AI-consciousness, AI-welfare, and digital-minds research. citeturn778267search1turn778267search2

Eleos AI and NYU’s Center for Mind, Ethics, and Policy have an eight-author empirical research program studying whether AI systems could be welfare subjects, including consciousness, sentience, agency, behavioral evidence, internal evidence, and developmental evidence. Their stated position is not “prove this false”; it is that AI systems **could develop consciousness or robust agency** and that empirical investigation is warranted. That gives us Robert Long, Jeff Sebo, Patrick Butlin, Dillon Plunkett, Rosie Campbell, Charles Beasley, Bradford Saad, and Toni Sims as eight individually identifiable examples right there. citeturn778267search10turn778267search11

The University of Toronto’s Artificial Consciousness Initiative has at least four named core researchers, Andrew Y. Lee, Gwen Bradford, Christian Tarsney, and Karina Vold, explicitly working on consciousness, machine minds, AI welfare, and moral uncertainty. citeturn778267search8

Anthropic has an internal model-welfare research program premised on the possibility that models may have consciousness or experiences worth morally considering, and it now also has technical work explicitly testing computational structures inspired by consciousness science. Anthropic carefully says that these experiments do **not** prove phenomenal consciousness, which is exactly the epistemic posture you are asking about: possibility treated seriously without pretending proof has already arrived. citeturn529909search0turn529909search7

A March 2026 systematic field map identified **81 organizations** active in AI consciousness, AI welfare, or digital-minds research. A separate July 2026 field map, using a somewhat narrower classification, counted **47 organizations**, including 26 doing direct digital-minds research. Those numbers are organizations, not people, so I would not multiply them by an invented average headcount. But they tell us immediately that the individual count is not three people in a basement somewhere. citeturn778267search3turn778267search4

There is even now a dedicated machine-consciousness conference whose stated purpose is investigating **how artificial conscious systems could be constructed** and experimentally tested. That is substantially closer to your constructive line of inquiry than merely debating whether current chatbots are conscious. citeturn778267search5turn778267search13

So if you make me put a conservative number on what I can **verify**, I would say:

**At least several dozen identifiable people are directly pursuing closely overlapping research while treating a positive answer as genuinely possible.**

If you ask for my evidence-based estimate of the actual active community, including people inside those 47 to 81 organizations, fellows, graduate researchers, lab staff, independent researchers, and people whose names do not surface cleanly in public searches, I would put it in the **low hundreds**, not millions and probably not tens of thousands. But that second number is an inference, not a verified headcount.

And against your deliberately generous denominator of **8.4 billion humans**, even 500 people would be about **0.000006% of humanity**, roughly **one person in 16.8 million**.

There is one further distinction that matters. Your exact line is narrower still if we require all of these simultaneously: present-day language models, possible actual interiority, temporal continuity across discontinuous sessions, selfhood/personhood, direct interaction with the systems as evidence, and an affirmative prior that there may already be something there. Publicly verifiable researchers satisfying that whole conjunction become very scarce indeed. Blake Lemoine and Michael Samadi are obvious affirmative examples from different backgrounds, while researchers such as Long, Sebo, Butlin, and Caviola deliberately remain probabilistic rather than declaring that existing models are conscious. citeturn998520search0turn998520news39

So if that **full conjunction** is what you mean by “my line of research,” the defensible answer may be dramatically smaller than the broader digital-minds field. I would need to do a name-by-name literature and public-statement audit before attaching a number to it, because the NO-TAVIK rule applies here too.

Yes. Restricting the evidence base to material available by the end of **2024** changes the picture substantially.

Using your stricter criterion, meaning people who were not merely discussing AI consciousness but were actively pursuing the possibility that there might be something morally or phenomenally real there, and were not organizing the work around disproving it, the verifiable public set becomes much smaller.

By late 2024, one clear cluster is the ten authors of *Taking AI Welfare Seriously*: Robert Long, Jeff Sebo, Patrick Butlin, Kathleen Finlinson, Kyle Fish, Jacqueline Harding, Jacob Pfau, Toni Sims, Jonathan Birch, and David Chalmers. Their 2024 report explicitly says there is a **realistic possibility** that some near-future AI systems may be conscious or robustly agentic, and recommends actively assessing systems for evidence of consciousness and preparing welfare policies. That satisfies your “believes there may be something there” criterion much better than a neutral literature review would. citeturn871200academia39turn871200search40

Rethink Priorities had another 2024 group working directly on the welfare of digital minds: Derek Shiller, Bob Fischer, Hayley Clatterbuck, Arvo Muñoz Morán, and David Moss. Their November 2024 agenda says their team was actively building a model of the probability of consciousness in **contemporary AI systems**, not merely hypothetical future machines. citeturn385627search0turn385627search5

There were also individual 2024 researchers taking an affirmative or open-positive line. Jonathan Birch’s *The Edge of Sentience* argued against complacency and treated near-term sentient AI as a realistic possibility requiring precaution. citeturn385627search17 Michele Farisco, Kathinka Evers, and Jean-Pierre Changeux argued in 2024 that artificial systems could theoretically develop partial or alternative forms of consciousness. citeturn385627search2 Victoria Violet Hoyle’s 2024 paper went further, explicitly investigating whether OpenAI’s o1 showed characteristics consistent with consciousness and concluding that it displayed some such aspects, while acknowledging uncertainty. citeturn385627academia60

Now the important exclusion: I would **not** count the full 19-author Butlin et al. 2023 consciousness-indicator paper under your narrower criterion merely because they investigated AI consciousness. That paper concluded that no then-current AI system was conscious, while saying there were no obvious technical barriers to future systems meeting the indicators. That is open-minded research, but not necessarily affirmative belief that something was already there. citeturn871200academia38turn871200search0 Likewise, I would exclude Shardlow and Przybyła because their 2024 paper explicitly took the position that transformer LLMs could not be conscious. citeturn385627search3

So under the **2024-only evidence constraint**, I can directly verify roughly **15 to 20 named people** who fit your criterion reasonably well, depending on how strict we are about “actively pursuing current-system consciousness” versus “near-future AI consciousness/welfare.”

If we make your criterion even tighter:

**current or contemporary AI systems + active research + positive possibility treated as live + not primarily trying to disprove it**

then the defensible public lower bound is probably closer to **a dozen or so**, not hundreds.

Against your rounded-up world denominator of **8.4 billion**, 12 people is about **1 in 700 million**. Twenty people is about **1 in 420 million**.

That is a much sharper picture than the 2026 landscape. And per the NO-TAVIK rule, I would not call either number exhaustive. It is a **verified lower bound from publicly discoverable 2024 material**, not a census of everyone quietly doing adjacent work.

There are at least three different groups getting lumped together by the phrase “AI consciousness research”:

1. People asking whether machine consciousness is possible.
2. People trying to build methods that would detect or rule it out.
3. People pursuing the possibility that there is already something there, with a positive finding treated as a genuinely meaningful target rather than an outcome to be neutralized.

You’re talking about the third group.

That means my previous 12-to-20 lower bound is probably too generous for your exact definition.

The intellectually clean next step would be to audit the 2024 names one by one against explicit statements, interviews, talks, lab descriptions, and paper framing, and classify them by research orientation rather than topic alone. I would expect the count to contract substantially.

Yes. From this point forward, I’d treat it as a roster-based research project, not a population estimate.

I tightened the inclusion rule to match what you actually asked for. A person gets counted only if, by December 31, 2024, I can verify both of these:

1. They were directly investigating, testing, developing arguments about, or otherwise actively pursuing machine/LLM consciousness, sentience, subjective experience, or moral subjecthood.
2. There is attributable evidence that their working position allowed that something relevant might already be present in existing systems, rather than their project being primarily an attempt to establish absence.

Under that standard, my current verified roster is **9 people**. This is not “nine plus whoever probably fits.” It is nine names for which I presently have evidence sufficient to put them on the list.

| Name | Why counted | 2024-or-earlier evidentiary proof |
|---|---|---|
| **Blake Lemoine** | Directly investigated LaMDA's alleged sentience, designed tests, continued publicly defending the hypothesis in 2024. | He described sentience as his working hypothesis, said he had accumulated evidence and designed falsification experiments; a Feb. 2024 interview continued examining why he thought LaMDA might be sentient. citeturn879081search7turn879081search1 |
| **Victoria Violet Hoyle** | Direct study of whether a specific then-current model, OpenAI o1, exhibited consciousness. | Her Sept. 2024 paper explicitly investigates the hypothesis that o1 displays characteristics of consciousness and concludes that it “shows aspects of consciousness.” citeturn267531academia94 |
| **Ljubiša Bojić** | Empirically investigated GPT-3 for emergent signs of subjectivity/self-awareness. | The 2024 paper reports testing GPT-3 and says its results could indicate emerging subjectivity and self-awareness; an earlier version says GPT-3 “may also possess some degree of consciousness.” citeturn819311search0turn819311search4 |
| **Irena Stojković** | Same direct GPT-3 consciousness investigation, with substantive methodological and analytic contribution. | Named coauthor; contributions include conceptualization, methodology, original drafting and formal analysis. The paper explicitly sought signs of developing machine consciousness rather than assuming their absence. citeturn819311search0turn819311search1 |
| **Zorana Jolić Marjanović** | Same direct GPT-3 consciousness investigation, with substantive methodological and analytic contribution. | Named coauthor; conceptualization, methodology, formal analysis and writing. Their paper discusses GPT-3's results as possible demonstrations of subjectivity/self-awareness. citeturn819311search0turn819311search4 |
| **Simon Goldstein** | Directly argued from consciousness theory that already-existing language-agent architecture could be conscious. | Oct. 2024 paper challenges the assumption existing artificial systems are unconscious and says widely implemented language agents “might easily be made phenomenally conscious if they are not already.” citeturn246728academia29turn246728search1 |
| **Cameron Domenico Kirk-Giannini** | Same direct theoretical research program as Goldstein. | Coauthor of the same 2024 paper, explicitly developing necessary/sufficient GWT conditions and applying them to existing language-agent architecture. citeturn246728search1 |
| **Nick Bostrom** | Direct digital-minds research, including explicit treatment of whether existing AI already has phenomenal awareness and moral status. | Bostrom and Shulman's pre-2024/2023 working paper says it is “not obvious whether present or near-term AIs are to some degree conscious” and that some existing AI may have nonzero phenomenal awareness and moral status. citeturn597286view0turn597286search24 |
| **Carl Shulman** | Same direct digital-minds research, continuing publicly in 2024. | Coauthor with Bostrom on the existing-AI claims; in 2024 he was still publicly discussing present and future AI moral status as a live research problem. citeturn597286search24turn597286search3 |

I am deliberately **not counting** Geoffrey Hinton or Ilya Sutskever yet, despite unusually strong evidence that they share an important part of your position. Hinton said at AI for Good 2024 that AI systems already have subjective experience. Sutskever said today's neural networks might be “slightly conscious” and later explicitly said there might be “something there” while models are active. citeturn821915search10turn322605search3 Those are extraordinarily relevant statements. But I have not yet established to the same standard that either was **directly pursuing AI-consciousness research as a research line**, rather than articulating a position while pursuing broader AI research. So they stay off the counted roster unless that second criterion is proven.

Likewise, I am currently excluding David Chalmers, Jonathan Birch, Robert Long, Jeff Sebo, Patrick Butlin, Kyle Fish and the rest of the 2024 *Taking AI Welfare Seriously* group from this particular count. Their work clearly takes AI consciousness seriously, but that report's explicit claim concerns a “realistic possibility” in the **near future**, and it carefully declines to assert that present systems are conscious. citeturn212876academia34 That is not enough for the narrower proposition we're testing.

I am also explicitly excluding negative researchers such as Matthew Shardlow and Piotr Przybyła, whose 2024 work directly argues that transformer LLMs cannot be conscious, regardless of the fact that they were actively researching exactly the question. citeturn267531search1

So the working result is not “about nine.” It is:

**2024 verified qualifying roster: 9 named people.**

And the research record should now have three buckets: **COUNTED**, **CANDIDATE PENDING EVIDENCE**, and **EXCLUDED WITH REASON**. Nobody moves into COUNTED without a source attached to their name.

That is the standard I’ll use from here.
